Refined description and stability for singular solutions of the 2D Keller-Segel system
Charles Collot, Tej-Eddine Ghoul, Nader Masmoudi, Van Tien Nguyen

Abstract
We construct solutions to the two dimensional parabolic-elliptic Keller-Segel model for chemotaxis that blow up in finite time T T . The solution is decomposed as the sum of a stationary state concentrated at scale λ \lambda and of a perturbation. We rely on a detailed spectral analysis for the linearised dynamics in the parabolic neighbourhood of the singularity performed by the authors in [ 10 ] , providing a refined expansion of the perturbation. Our main result is the construction of a stable dynamics in the full nonradial setting for which the stationary state collapses with the universal law λ ∼ 2 ​ e − 2 + γ 2 ​ T − t ​ e − | ln ⁡ ( T − t ) | 2 \lambda\sim 2e^{-\frac{2+\gamma}{2}}\sqrt{T-t}e^{-\sqrt{\frac{|\ln(T-t)|}{2}}} where γ \gamma is the Euler constant. This improves on the earlier result by Raphael and Schweyer [ 51 ] and gives a new robust approach to so-called type II singularities for critical parabolic problems. A by-product of the spectral analysis we developed is the existence of unstable blowup dynamics with speed λ ℓ ∼ C 0 ​ ( T − t ) ℓ 2 ​ | ln ⁡ ( T − t ) | − ℓ 2 ​ ( ℓ − 1 ) \lambda_{\ell}\sim C_{0}(T-t)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}|\ln(T-t)|^{-\frac{\ell}{2(\ell-1)}} for ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 integer.



1. Introduction

1.1. The Keller-Segel system

This paper is concerned with the Keller-Segel system modelling chemotaxis.
Chemotaxis
is a biological phenomenon
describing the change of motion of a population density (of certain cells, animals, and of particles as well) in response (
taxis
) to an external chemical stimulus spread in the environment where they reside. The chemical signal can be secreted by the species itself or supplied to it by an external source. As a consequence, the species changes its movement toward (
positive chemotaxis
) or away from (
negative chemotaxis
) a higher concentration of the chemical substance. A possible fascinating issue of a positive chemotactical movement is the aggregation of the organisms involved to form a more complex organism or body. The first mathematical model for chemotaxis was proposed by Keller-Segel
[
28
]
to describe the aggregation of the slime mold amoebae Dictyostelium discoideum (see also Patlak
[
48
]
for an earlier model and
[
30
]
,
[
29
]
,
[
27
]
for various assessments). Since the publication of
[
28
]
, a large literature has addressed the mathematical, biological and medical aspects of
chemotaxis
, showing the importance of the problem and the great interest that different kinds of scientists carry on it. We recommend the reference
[
25
]
for a survey of the mathematical problems encountered in the study of the Keller-Segel model and also a wide bibliography, including references on other types of models describing
chemotaxis
.

The present paper deals with a simplified version of the Keller-Segel model introduced by Nanjundiah
[
47
]
, which reads as follows:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{t}u&=\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla\Phi_{u}),\\
0&=\Delta\Phi_{u}+u,\end{array}\right.\quad(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(1)

Here
u
represents the cell density, and
\Phi_{u}
is the
concentration of chemoattractant which can be defined directly by

\Phi_{u}(x,t)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log|x-y|u(y,t)dy.

(2)

The nonlinear term
\nabla.(u\nabla\Phi_{u})
models the cell movement towards higher concentrations of the chemical signal. The more the cells aggregate, the more is the attracting chemical substance produced by the cells. This process is counterbalanced by cell diffusion, but if the cell density is sufficiently large, the nonlocal chemical interaction counterbalances the diffusion and results in a blowup of the cell density. The solution blows up in finite time
T
in the sense that

\limsup_{t\to T}\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=+\infty,

and the blowup set
\mathcal{S}
is then defined by

\mathcal{S}=\{\hat{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid\exists(x_{k},t_{k})\to(\hat{a},T)\;\text{such that}\;|u(x_{k},t_{k})|\to+\infty\}.

(3)

Solutions of system (
1
) satisfy the conservation of the total mass as well as the positivity of the cell density,

M:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,t)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}(x)dx,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mbox{if }u_{0}\geq 0\ \mbox{ then }\ u(t)\geq 0.

(4)

There is also a scaling invariance: if
u
is a solution then so is the rescaled function

u_{\lambda}(x,t)=\lambda^{-2}u(\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda^{-2}t),\quad\forall\lambda>0.

As the mass which is a conserved quantity is invariant under the above renormalisation, the problem is called critical. In two space dimensions, it was first proved (see Jäger-Luckhaus
[
26
]
, Corrias-Perthame-Zaag
[
13
]
) that there is global existence for solutions with small initial mass, while blowup occurs for a large initial mass. The existence of a mass threshold was then conjectured in
[
47
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
7
]
, namely that the chemotactic collapse (blowup) should occur if and only if
M
is greater than
8\pi
. This
8\pi
mass threshold conjecture is later proven in
[
18
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
3
]
(see also
[
41
]
,
[
42
]
for related results in the bounded domain case). Following
[
3
]
, the criticality of the mass value can be seen by computing the second moment

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}u(x,t)dx=4M\left(1-\frac{M}{8\pi}\right).

(5)

Thus, if
M>8\pi
, the right hand side is strictly negative, and positive solutions with finite second moment cannot be globally defined, or this second moment would reach zero in finite time.

Below the threshold
M<8\pi
, Dolbeault-Perthame announced in
[
19
]
that there is global existence of a solution for system (
1
) in a weak sense. This result is further completed and improved in
[
5
]
,
[
3
]
through the existence of
free-energy solutions
. Furthermore, the asymptotic behavior is given by a unique self-similar profile of the system (see also
[
45
]
for radially symmetric results concerning self-similar behavior).

At the threshold
M=8\pi
, the authors of
[
1
,
2
]
show the existence of global radially symmetric solutions to system (
1
) for initial data with finite or infinite second moment. In
[
4
]
, Blanchet-Carrillo-Masmoudi proved the existence of solutions to (
1
) concentrating in infinite time through the
free energy functional
introduced by Nagai-Senbai-Yoshida in
[
43
]
. Furthermore, they showed that the solution converges to a delta Dirac distribution at the center of mass.

The system (
1
) has a family of explicit stationary solutions of the form

\forall\lambda>0,\;a\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad U_{\lambda,a}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-a}{\lambda}\right)\quad\text{with}\quad U(x)=\frac{8}{(1+|x|^{2})^{2}}.

(6)

These solutions have the threshold mass
M=8\pi
and infinite second moment. They play an important role in the description of concentration both in finite and infinite time. Ghoul-Masmoudi
[
21
]
construct concrete infinite time blowup solutions to (
1
) with threshold mass
M=8\pi
admitting the asymptotic dynamic as
t\to+\infty
,

u(x,t)\sim U_{\lambda(t)}(x)e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{2t}}\quad\textup{with}\quad\lambda^{2}(t)\sim\frac{I}{\ln t}\;\;\textup{and}\;\;I=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}u_{0}(x)dx,

see also Davila-del Pino-Dolbeault-Musso-Wei
[
14
]
for an entirely different approach from that of
[
21
]
that leads to the same blowup rate.

It is worth to mention that the study of the positive steady states of the problem (
1
), namely the solutions of the elliptic system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&=\Delta u-\nabla.(u\nabla\Phi_{u}),\\
0&=\Delta\Phi_{u}+u,\end{array}\right.\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\;\;u>0,

(7)

is equivalent to the study of the ground states of the equation

\Delta v+\lambda_{0}e^{v}=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\lambda_{0}>0.

(8)

This basic feature observed in
[
17
]
follows from the fact that the solution of (
7
) satisfies the relation

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u|\nabla(\log u-\Phi_{u})|^{2}dx=0,

so that
u=\lambda e^{\Phi_{u}}
for some positive constant
\lambda
, resulting in equation (
8
). Note that
V_{\lambda,a}(x)=\log U_{\lambda,a}(x),
where
U_{\lambda,a}
is defined by (
6
), is a solution to (
8
) with
\lambda_{0}=1
. The asymptotic behaviour of solutions of (
8
), in a bounded domain
\Omega
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or in the whole space for which
\lambda_{0}\int_{\Omega}e^{v}
remains uniformly bounded, is well understood after the works by Brezis -Merle
[
6
]
, Nagasaki-Suzuki
[
44
]
, Li-Shafrir
[
33
]
, Manuel-Wei
[
17
]
and references therein. Their results read as follows:
\lambda_{0}e^{v}
approaches a superposition of Dirac deltas in the interior of
\Omega
.
More precisely, the authors in
[
17
]
show that for all
\lambda_{0}
sufficiently small, there exists a solution
v_{\lambda_{0}}
to equation (
8
) such that

v_{\lambda_{0}}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}V_{\mu_{i}\sqrt{\lambda_{0}},a_{i}}(x)+\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\int_{\Omega}e^{v_{\lambda_{0}}(x)}dx\to 8\pi m\quad\text{as}\quad\lambda_{0}\to 0,

where
V_{\mu_{i}\sqrt{\lambda_{0}},a_{i}}
is defined above, and the
a_{i}
’s are the local maxima of
v_{\lambda_{0}}
in the interior of
\Omega
and the
\mu_{i}
’s are the positive constants.

Above the threshold
M>8\pi
, concrete examples of finite time blowup solutions are constructed by Herrero-Velázquez in
[
24
]
(the scaling law found there is false but after correcting it the rest of the proof remains valid), with a further stability study in
[
57
,
59
,
58
]
and by Raphäel-Schweyer
[
51
]
. Regarding the temporal blowup rate, the central issue is to distinguish type I from type II blowup. We say that a solution
u(t)
of (
1
) exhibits type I blowup at
t=T
if there exist a constant
C>0
such that

\limsup_{t\to T}(T-t)\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C,

(9)

otherwise, the blowup is of type II. This notion is motivated by the ODE
u_{t}=u^{2}
obtained by discarding diffusion and transport in the equation. The lower blowup rate estimate

\|u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\geq C(T-t)^{-1}

is obtained for any blowup solutions of (
1
) by Kozono-Sugiyama
[
31
]
. Importantly, it is known that in the two dimensional case any blowup solution of (
1
) is of type II (see Theorem 8.19 in
[
56
]
and Theorem 10 in
[
46
]
for such a statement). In
[
55
]
, Suzuki studies the problem (
1
) in a bounded domain, with Dirichlet condition for the Poisson part, i.e.
\Phi_{u}\mid_{\partial\Omega}=0
, so that the blowup is excluded on the boundary. More precisely, he proves that

u(x,t)\rightharpoonup\sum_{\hat{a}\in\mathcal{S}}m(\hat{a})\delta_{\hat{a}}(dx)+f(x)dx\quad\text{in}\;\;\mathcal{M}(\bar{\Omega})=\mathcal{C}(\bar{\Omega})^{\prime}

(10)

as
t\to T
, where
0<f(x)\in L^{1}(\Omega)\cap\mathcal{C}(\Omega\setminus\mathcal{S})
. Furthermore, the author also asserts that
m(\hat{a})=8\pi
holds for each
\hat{a}\in\Omega\cap\mathcal{S}
.

1.2. Statement of the result

Singularity formation for critical problems has attracted a great amount of work since the seminal results for dispersive equations by Merle-Raphael
[
36
]
, Krieger-Schlag-Tataru
[
32
]
, Rodnianski-Sterbenz
[
53
]
, Raphael-Rodnianski
[
49
]
and references therein. The approach of
[
36
]
,
[
49
]
,
[
37
]
, relying on a careful understanding of the solution near the stationary state, and on modulation laws computed via so-called tail dynamics, has been carried on to parabolic problems
[
50
,
52
,
51
]
,
[
54
]
and
[
9
]
. Type II singularities for the semilinear heat equation had been previously studied by means of matched asymptotic expansions in an unpublished paper by Herrero-Velazquez (see
[
23
]
for an announcement of their result and
[
20
]
for a formal analysis). This result was later confirmed by Mizoguchi
[
39
,
40
]
, and a new inner-outer gluing technique developed recently by Davila-del Pino-Wei
[
15
]
(see also
[
16
]
and references therein for recent results). A new approach for the construction of singular solutions of parabolic problems was initiated in
[
22
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
38
]
and the present paper fits into this series of works. The aim is to study type II blow-up as well as self-similar singularities, for supercritical and critical equations, in a unified and more natural approach (see first comment below). The present paper aims at applying for the first time this new approach to the delicate degenerate problem of the critical collapse for the parabolic-elliptic Keller-Segel problem (
1
). In comparison with
[
51
]
, we obtain a refined expansion for the scale (proving the precise universal law (
12
)), the nonradial stability of the dynamics, the existence of unstable blow-up laws and remove the slightly supercritical mass restriction (
M
close to
8\pi
). The solutions we construct are in the following function space

\mathcal{E}:=\left\{u:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\|u\|_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}:=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle x\rangle^{\frac{3}{2}+2k}|\nabla^{k}u|^{2}<\infty\right\}.

(11)

Theorem 1.1

(Stable blowup solutions)
.

There exists a set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{E}\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of initial data
u_{0}
such that the following holds for the associated solution to (
1
). It blows up in finite time
T=T(u_{0})>0
according to the dynamic

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}(t)}\left(U+\tilde{u}\right)\left(\frac{x-x^{*}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right),

where

•

(Precise law for the scale)

\lambda(t)=2e^{-\frac{2+\gamma}{2}}\sqrt{T-t}e^{-\sqrt{\frac{|\ln T-t|}{2}}}(1+o_{t\uparrow T}(1)).

(12)

•

(Convergence of the blow-up point)
There exists
X=X(u_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
x^{*}(t)\rightarrow X
as
t\uparrow T
.

•

(Convergence to the stationary state profile)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\tilde{u}^{2}(t,y)+\langle y\rangle^{2}|\nabla\tilde{u}(t,y)|^{2}\right)dy\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as }t\uparrow T.

(13)

•

(Stability)
For any
u_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
, there exists
\delta(u_{0})>0
such that if
v_{0}\in\mathcal{E}\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfies
\|v_{0}-u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\leq\delta(u_{0})
then
v_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
and the same conclusions hold true for the corresponding solution
v
.

•

(Continuity)
For any fixed
u_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
, one has
(T(v_{0}),X(v_{0}))\rightarrow(T(u_{0}),X(u_{0}))
as
\|v_{0}-u_{0}\|_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow 0
.

Remark 1.2
.

In Theorem
1.1
, the initial datum
u_{0}
can possibly be non-radial, and possibly sign-changing. The exponent
3/2
in the definition (
11
) of the function space
\mathcal{E}
allows for initial data that are arbitrarily large in
L^{1}
(but sufficiently spread out away from the singularity). Additionally, our proof involves a detailed understanding of the perturbation
\tilde{u}
, see Definition
3.3
.

We are also able to construct for problem (
7
) blowup solutions having other unstable blowup speeds by the same analysis. This corresponds to the case where the leading order part of the perturbation is located on an eigenmode with faster decay, while the eigenmodes with slower decay are not excited. This is only obtained here in the radial case. The corresponding solutions are sign-changing.

Theorem 1.3

(Unstable blowup solutions)
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell\geq 2
, there exists an initial datum
u_{0}\in\mathcal{E}\cap L^{1}
with spherical symmetry, such that the corresponding solution to (
1
) blows up in finite time
T>0
according to the dynamic

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}(t)}\left(U+\tilde{u}\right)\left(\frac{x}{\lambda(t)}\right),

where

\lambda(t)\sim C(u_{0})(T-t)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}|\ln(T-t)|^{-\frac{\ell}{2(\ell-1)}},

(14)

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\tilde{u}^{2}(t,y)+\langle y\rangle^{2}|\nabla\tilde{u}(t,y)|^{2}\right)dy\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as }t\uparrow T.

Remark 1.4
.

We only give a complete proof of Theorem
1.1
, and sketch how it can be adapted to derive the conclusion of Theorem
1.3
. Only one major issue arises. These unstable blow-ups are related with eigenmodes of the linearized dynamics that decay faster. Since we do not control the constant (giving the decay rate) in the nonradial coercivity estimate of Proposition
2.4
, we are hence only able to construct such unstable blow-ups in the radial sector.

Comment 1.5

(A robust approach)
.

Parabolic type II singularities involve two-scale problems. To leading order the solution is given by a stationary state that is concentrated at a scale that is much smaller than the parabolic scale (i.e., distance
\sqrt{T-t}
from the singularity). The core of our approach, following
[
22
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
11
]
and
[
38
]
, is to obtain the local expansion of the perturbation in the parabolic neighbourhood of the singularity, relying on a precise spectral analysis. The interactions between the stationary state and its perturbation are understood via projecting the dynamics on the corresponding eigenmodes, yielding the obtention of the scaling law. This allows for the construction of a detailed approximate solution, and gives a functional framework to control the remainder,
simultaneously at the scale of the stationary state and at the parabolic scale
. Previous works such as
[
51
]
controlled very accurately the solution near the stationary state, but less in the parabolic zone which implied for example the use of what they call radiations and modified modulation equations.

Let us now comment on other available techniques. The inner-outer gluing method, developed in
[
15
]
for the study of singularity formation for the two-dimensional harmonic map flow, provides an interesting alternative framework to ours; the perturbation is controlled separately close to the stationary state and in the parabolic zone, relying on parabolic estimates and on fixed point arguments. The control of the perturbation is obtained under suitable orthogonality conditions, which yields the desired scaling law. Another approach in
[
23
]
and
[
39
,
40
]
for the semilinear heat equation used intersection numbers argument, which are unfortunately not available in the nonradial case. We believe the spectral argument in our technique is the starting point for further studies, such as the possible classification of blowup rates in the nonradial case, and our framework would be adapted to the case of semi-linear hyperbolic equations as well.

Comment 1.6

(Main novelties)
.

There are important novelties in the present work, and it is enlightening to compare with the one of
[
11
]
. The common approach is the analysis of the linearised operator around a stationary state that is concentrated at a scale smaller than the parabolic scale, here (
17
). This spectral problem is solved in Proposition
2.1
(obtained in
[
10
]
). The perturbation of the stationary state is then expanded along the eigenmodes and the precise knowledge of the eigenvalues then allows for the computation of the scaling laws, here (
12
) and (
14
). However, in the present critical case the aforementioned spectral problem is degenerate. Indeed, the concentrated stationary state at scale
\nu\rightarrow 0
gives a singular limit (the operator does not converge to its pointwise limit). The stable blow-up law corresponds moreover to a degenerate eigenvalue that is
0
to leading order, and one needs to go to next order corrections that are of order
1/|\log\nu|
and
1/|\log\nu|^{2}
instead of being polynomial.

The nonlinear analysis is more involved too, as we cannot rely on signed quantities or parameters restriction (respectively equation (1.8) and Lemma A.1 in
[
11
]
). In the part of the analysis that establishes upper bounds on the perturbation, certain nonlinear terms cannot be treated as lower order in comparison with linear terms (i.e. considered as forcing terms for the linearised evolution). We then put them as part of the linearised operator, and show that the spectral structure remains true to leading order (Lemma
4.2
). This uses as a key feature that the nonlinear terms, due to their very algebraical form in (
1
), are orthogonal to the resonance of the linear operator near the stationary state (see
[
10
]
). Moreover, to deal with the nonradial part of the solution we use a new coercivity estimate (
78
), which includes the scaling term in the linearised operator, what greatly simplifies our analysis.

Comment 1.7

(Extension of the stability analysis)
.

Our analysis relies on the description and control of the solution
near
the singularity, which is almost decoupled from what happens
away
from it. Indeed, first, the key part of the analysis takes place in a space with exponentially decaying weight in the zone
|x-x^{*}|\lesssim\sqrt{T-t}
, second, the treatment of the exterior part allows for cutting of the
full
solution at distance
|x-x^{*}|\gtrsim 1
from the singularity. The present analysis could be easily adapted to prove the following two results. First, the analogue of Theorem
1.1
for the existence of this stable blowup dynamics at a point inside a smooth bounded domain, second the existence of multiple stationary states blowing up simultaneously as described in Theorem
1.1
at
n
distinct points for any
n\geq 2
.

Notations.

Throughout this paper, we use the notation
A\lesssim B
to say that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
0\leq A\leq CB
. Similarly,
A\sim B
means that there exist constants
0<c<C
such that
cA\leq B\leq CA
. We denote by

\left<r\right>=\sqrt{1+r^{2}}

the Japanese bracket. Let
\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a cut-off function with
0\leq\chi\leq 1
,
\chi(x)=1
for
|x|\leq 1
and
\chi(x)=0
for
|x|\geq 2
. We define for all
M>0
,

\chi_{{}_{M}}(x)=\chi\Big(\frac{x}{M}\Big).

(15)

Given
\nu>0
and a function
f
, we introduce:

f_{\nu}(z)=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}f\Big(\frac{z}{\nu}\Big),\quad y=\frac{z}{\nu},\quad\zeta=|z|,\quad r=|y|.

We introduce the differential operator

\Lambda f(z)=\frac{d}{d\nu}\big[f_{\nu}(z)\big]_{\nu=1}=\nabla\cdot(zf)=2f+z\cdot\nabla f,

(16)

and the linearized operator around the scaled stationary solution
U_{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}^{z}f(z)=\mathscr{L}^{z}_{0}f-\beta\Lambda f,

(17)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}^{z}f=\nabla\cdot(U_{\nu}\nabla\mathscr{M}^{z}f)\quad\textup{with}\quad\mathscr{M}^{z}f=\frac{f}{U_{\nu}}-\Phi_{f}.

(18)

In the partial mass setting, namely for

m_{f}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{|z|\leq\zeta}f(z)dz,

the operator
\mathscr{L}^{z}
acting on radially symmetric functions is transformed to

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}=\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}_{0}-\beta\zeta\partial_{\zeta},

(19)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}_{0}=\partial_{\zeta}^{2}-\frac{1}{\zeta}\partial_{\zeta}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}(Q_{\nu}\cdot)}{\zeta}\quad\textup{and}\quad Q_{\nu}(\zeta)=\frac{4\zeta^{2}}{\zeta^{2}+\nu^{2}}.

(20)

In terms of the
y
-variable, we work with its rescaled versions

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}f(y)=\mathscr{L}_{0}f-\beta\nu^{2}\Lambda f,

(21)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}f=\nabla\cdot(U\nabla\mathscr{M}f)\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathscr{M}f=\frac{f}{U}-\Phi_{f}.

(22)

In the partial mass setting the linear operator
\mathscr{L}
becomes

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}_{0}-\beta\nu^{2}r\partial_{r},

(23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{0}=\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{\partial_{r}(Q\cdot)}{r}\quad\textup{and}\quad Q(r)=\frac{4r^{2}}{1+r^{2}}.

(24)

We also introduce the weight functions

\displaystyle\omega_{\nu}(z)=\frac{\nu^{2}}{U_{\nu}(z)}e^{-\frac{\beta|z|^{2}}{2}},\quad\rho_{0}(z)=e^{-\frac{\beta|z|^{2}}{2}},

(25)

\displaystyle\omega(y)=\frac{1}{U(y)}e^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}|y|^{2}}{2}},\quad\rho(y)=e^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}|y|^{2}}{2}}.

(26)

The partial mass of the solution is formally a radial solution in dimension
0
, so that to take
k
adapted derivatives we will use the notation
D^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where

D^{2k}=\left(\zeta\partial_{\zeta}(\frac{\partial_{\zeta}}{\zeta})\right)^{2k},\ \ D^{2k+1}=\partial_{\zeta}D^{2k}

and the notation for integers modulo
2
,

k\wedge 2=k\mod 2.

For a function
f
of a variable
\xi
representing any variable in the problem, the radial and non-radial parts of
f
are defined as:

f(\xi)=f^{0}(\xi)+f^{\perp}(\xi),\qquad f^{0}(\xi)=(2\pi|\xi|)^{-1}\int_{S(0,|\xi|)}f(\tilde{\xi})dS.

1.3. Strategy of the proof

We briefly explain the main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.1
and sketch the different points in the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Renormalization and linearization of the problem:
The essential part of the analysis lies in the parabolic zone
|x-X|\lesssim\sqrt{T-t}
. Since neither
X
nor
T
are known a priori, our method will compute them
dynamically
. In view of the scaling invariance of the problem (
1
), we introduce the change of variables

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}}w(z,\tau),\quad\Phi_{u}(x,t)=\Phi_{w}(z,\tau),\quad z=\frac{x-x^{*}}{\mu},\quad\frac{d\tau}{dt}=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}},

(27)

where
\mu(t)
and
x^{*}(t)
are time dependent parameters to be fixed later. They will in fine satisfy
-\mu_{\tau}/\mu\to\beta_{\infty}>0
,
\mu(t)\sim\sqrt{2\beta_{\infty}(T-t)}
(see (
247
)) and
x^{*}\to X
, so that
z
is indeed the parabolic variable. The equation satisfied by
w
is:

\partial_{\tau}w=\nabla\cdot(\nabla w-w\nabla\Phi_{w})-\beta\nabla\cdot(zw)+\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla w\quad\textup{with}\quad\beta=-\frac{\mu_{\tau}}{\mu}.

(28)

There is no Type I blowup solutions for the problem (
1
) in the sense of (
9
). Thus, our goal is to construct an unbounded global-in-time solution
w(z,\tau)
for equation (
28
). In particular, we construct a solution of the form

w(z,\tau)=U_{\nu}(z)+\eta(z,\tau),

where
\nu(\tau)
is the main parameter function in our analysis which drives the law of blowup, and
\eta
solves the
linearized equation in the parabolic zone

\partial_{\tau}\eta=\mathscr{L}^{z}\eta+\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\right)\Lambda U_{\nu}-\nabla\cdot\Big(\eta\Phi_{\eta}\Big)+\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}.\nabla\left(U_{\nu}+\eta\right)

(29)

Here,
\mathscr{L}^{z}
is the linearized operator defined by (
17
) . Our aim is then reduced to construct for equation (
29
) a global-in-time solution
\eta(z,\tau)
satisfying (
13
).

Properties of the linearized operator:
We expect that only the first two terms contribute to leading order in the right hand side of (
29
). In the radial setting, studying the operator
\mathscr{L}^{z}
is equivalent to studying
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
, the linearised operator around
Q_{\nu}=m_{U_{\nu}}
in the partial mass setting defined by (
19
). Indeed, we have the relation

\mathscr{L}^{z}f(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\zeta}\partial_{\zeta}\Big(\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}m_{f}(\zeta)\Big).

(30)

In the regime
0<\beta\nu^{2}\ll 1
, we proved in
[
10
]
that
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
with compact resolvant (see Proposition
2.1
for a precise statement), its spectrum being:

\textup{spec}(\mathscr{A}^{\zeta})=\Big\{\alpha_{n}=2\beta\big(1-n+\frac{1}{2\ln\nu}+\bar{\alpha}_{n}\big),\;\bar{\alpha}_{n}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\right),\;n\in\mathbb{N}\Big\}.

(31)

(a refinement of
\bar{\alpha}_{0}
and
\bar{\alpha}_{1}
up to an accuracy of order
1/|\ln\nu|^{2}
is needed to derive a precise blowup rate). The eigenfunction
\phi_{n,\nu}
of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\alpha_{n}
is explicit to leading order, of the form

\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{n,j}\beta^{j}\nu^{2j-2}T_{j}\big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\big)+\tilde{\phi}_{n,\nu},\quad c_{n,j}=2^{j}\frac{n!}{(n-j)!}.

(32)

Above,
\tilde{\phi}_{n,\nu}
is of smaller order, and
(T_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
are defined by
T_{j+1}=-\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}T_{j}
and
T_{0}(r)=\frac{r^{2}}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}=m_{\Lambda U}
. The resonance of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
is
T_{0}
:
\mathscr{A}_{0}T_{0}=0
, so they generate the generalised kernel of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
. They admit the following asymptotic at infinity

\textup{for}\;\;j\geq 1,\quad T_{j}(r)\sim\hat{d}_{j}r^{2j-2}\ln r\quad\textup{with}\quad\hat{d}_{j+1}=-\frac{\hat{d}_{j}}{4j(j+1)},\quad\hat{d}_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}.

(33)

Moreover, the following spectral gap estimate holds: for
g\in L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
in the domain of
\mathcal{A}^{\zeta}
with
g\perp\phi_{n,\nu}
in
L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
for
0\leq j\leq N
,

\big\langle g,\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}g\big\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\leq\alpha_{N+1}\big\|g\big\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}.

(34)

On the non-radial sector, we also prove in
[
10
]
that the slightly modified linear operator
\tilde{\mathscr{L}}
defined by

\tilde{\mathscr{L}}u=\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla\Phi_{U})-\nabla\cdot(U\nabla\tilde{\Phi}_{u})-b\nabla\cdot(yu),\quad\tilde{\Phi}_{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}(-\Delta)^{-1}(u\sqrt{\rho}),

is coercive for the following well-adapted scalar product

\langle u,v\rangle_{\ast}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\sqrt{\rho}\mathscr{M}(v\sqrt{\rho})dy\quad\textup{with}\quad\mathscr{M}u=\frac{u}{U}-\Phi_{u}

(35)

(equivalent in norm to
L^{2}_{\omega_{0}}
under suitable orthogonality conditions). We show that for
u
without radial component with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\partial_{i}U\sqrt{\rho}dy=0
for
i=1,2
:

\big\langle u,\tilde{\mathscr{L}}u\big\rangle_{\ast}\leq-\delta_{0}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\omega}}

(36)

for a constant
\delta_{0}>0
. The advantage of this coercivity is that the scaling term
b\nabla\cdot(y\cdot)
is taken into account, which greatly simplifies our analysis for the nonradial part. Note that controling the scaling term is one of the difficulties in the analysis performed in
[
51
]
where the renormalized operators of
\mathscr{L}_{0},\mathscr{M}
and the dissipation structure of the problem together with a sharp control of tails at infinity play a crucial role in their analysis. A similar situation happens in many other critical blowup problems, see for example
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
37
]
,
[
50
]
,
[
52
]
,
[
49
]
, etc.

Approximate solution and a formal derivation of the blowup rate:
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\gg 1
, and consider the approximate solution to (
28
) of the form

\displaystyle W=W[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](z,\tau)

\displaystyle=U_{\nu}(\zeta)+a_{1}(\tau)\big(\varphi_{1,\nu}(\zeta)-\varphi_{0,\nu}(\zeta)\big)+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\varphi_{n,\nu}(\zeta),

where
\nu(\tau)
,
\mathbf{a}(\tau)=\big(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\big)(\tau)
and
\beta(\tau)
are parameters to be determined, and the
\varphi_{n,\nu}
’s are the radial eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{L}^{z}
. In the partial mass setting this gives

m_{W}[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](\zeta,\tau)=Q_{\nu}(\zeta)+a_{1}(\tau)(\phi_{1,\nu}(\zeta)-\phi_{0,\nu}(\zeta)\big)+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta).

(37)

Here, the term
a_{1}(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu})
is the main perturbation term driving the law of blowup speed; and the term
\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}\phi_{n,\nu}
is a higher order perturbation added to produce a big constant in the spectral gap (
34
), which is used to close the
L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
estimate for the radial part of the remainder. The generated error
m_{E}
(defined by (
98
)) from the approximate solution (
37
) is of size

\|m_{E}\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}.

(38)

Assuming temporarily that
W
is an exact solution to (
1
), after an appropriate projection of (
1
) onto
\phi_{n,\nu}
for
n=0,...,N
, we end up with the dynamical system (see Lemma
4.1
for more details):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l l }\textup{Mod}_{0}&:=&\nu^{2}\Big(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\Big)+\beta\frac{a_{1}}{4}(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}-1-\tilde{\alpha}_{0})=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\right),\\
\textup{Mod}_{1}&:=&a_{1,\tau}-\beta a_{1}\left(\frac{1}{\ln\nu}+\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-2-\ln\beta}{2|\ln\nu|^{2}}\right)+a_{1}\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\right),\\
\textup{Mod}_{n}&:=&a_{n,\tau}-2\beta a_{n}\alpha_{n}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\right)\quad\textup{for}\quad 2\leq n\leq N.\end{array}\right.

The first equation describes how the leading term in the perturbation forces the stationary state to shrink, and the second one how the leading term in the perturbation evolves. We now fix the parameter
\beta
by setting

\frac{a_{1}}{4\nu^{2}}=-1+\frac{1}{2\ln\nu}+\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-1-\ln\beta}{4|\ln\nu|^{2}}.

(39)

In this case, the first equation reduces to:

\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}=\beta\Big[-\frac{1}{2|\ln\nu|}+\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-1-\ln\beta}{4|\ln\nu|^{2}}\Big]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\right),

(40)

where
\gamma
is the Euler constant appearing in the refinement of
\bar{\alpha}_{0},\bar{\alpha}_{1}
(see Proposition
2.1
). Solving this equation gives

\nu(\tau)\sim Ae^{-\sqrt{\frac{\tau}{2}}}\quad\textup{with}\quad A=\sqrt{2/\beta_{\infty}}e^{-\frac{\gamma+2}{2}},

from which
\tau\sim|\ln(T-t)|
and
\mu(t)\sim\sqrt{2\beta_{\infty}(T-t)}
where
T=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}t(\tau)
, and we derive the blowup rate as stated in Theorem
1.1
. The identity (
39
) ensured
\nu\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow T
, hence is dynamical way to compute the blow-up time. Our derivation of the blowup rate is consistent with the formal analysis in
[
24
]
by means of matched asymptotic expansions. We would lile to emphasise the fact that the refinement of the first two eigenvalues up to an accuracy of order
\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}
is crucial in deriving the precise value of
A
here. Note that the rigorous analysis in
[
51
]
could not give the value of
A
.

Decomposition of the solution and modulation equations:
To produce a solution of the full nonlinear problem we decompose the solution as

w(z,\tau)=W[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](\zeta)+\varepsilon(z,\tau),\;m_{w}(\zeta,\tau)=m_{W}[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](\zeta)+m_{\varepsilon}(\zeta,\tau).

(41)

The uniqueness of this decomposition is ensured by the orthogonality conditions

\big\langle m_{\varepsilon},\phi_{n,\nu}\big\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=0\quad\textup{for}\;\;0\leq n\leq N,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{\perp}\nabla U_{\nu}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}dz=0.

(42)

The control of the radial part of
\varepsilon
is done via the partial mass setting, i.e.
m_{\varepsilon}
, based on the spectral properties of the linear operator
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
, and the control of the nonradial part
\varepsilon^{\perp}
of
\varepsilon
is based on the coercivity estimate (
36
). Here,
m_{\varepsilon}
and
\varepsilon^{\perp}
solve the equations (where
P_{\nu}=a_{1}(\varphi_{1,\nu}-\varphi_{0,\nu})+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}\varphi_{n,\nu}
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}m_{\varepsilon}=\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}m_{\varepsilon}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}((2P_{\nu}+m_{\varepsilon})m_{\varepsilon})}{2\zeta}+m_{E}+N_{0}(\varepsilon^{\perp}),

(43)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\varepsilon^{\perp}=\mathscr{L}^{z}\varepsilon^{\perp}-\nabla\cdot\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon^{\perp})+\frac{x_{\tau}^{*}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla(W+\varepsilon^{0})+N^{\perp}(\varepsilon^{\perp}),

(44)

where
m_{E}
is the generated error estimated in (
38
),
\nabla\cdot\mathcal{G}(\varepsilon^{\perp})
contains small linear terms, and
N_{0}
and
N^{\perp}
stand for higher order quadratic nonlinear terms corresponding to the projections on radial and nonradial modes. After projecting the above equations on suitable directions, we arrive at the full modulation equations (see Lemma
4.1
for complete expressions)

\displaystyle|\textup{Mod}_{0}|+|\textup{Mod}_{1}|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\|m_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}+\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\Big),\qquad|\textup{Mod}_{n}|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|}\|m_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}+\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\Big),

(45)

and (see (
107
) and Lemma
4.10
for more details)

\left|\frac{x_{\tau}^{*}}{\mu}\right|\lesssim\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{\nu}}}.

(46)

Control of the remainder:
In view of (
45
), the main quantity we need to control is the
L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
-norm of
m_{\varepsilon}
, which is of size (based on the error generated by the approximate solution, see (
38
))

\|m_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|},

(47)

so that the leading order dynamical system (
40
) driving the law of blowup still holds up to an accuracy of order
\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}
. At the linear lever, i.e. without taking into account the nonlinear term in (
43
), it’s simple to achieve (
47
) thanks to the spectral gap estimate (
34
). However, the only spectral gap (
34
) is not enough to control nonlinear terms directly and to close the estimate (
47
). Indeed, the perturbation
\varepsilon
can be large near the origin, and the sole
L^{2}_{\omega}
orthogonality conditions for it do not allow for dissipation type estimates there. Our idea is to put back certain nonlinear terms in the linearised operator and to prove that the spectral gap (
34
) still holds true for this perturbation. We slightly modify the decomposition (
41
) and extract the leading order part of
\varepsilon
near the origin:

m_{w}(\zeta,\tau)=Q_{\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)+a_{1}\big(\phi_{1,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)-\phi_{0,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)\big)+\tilde{m}_{w},

(48)

where we introduce the new parameter function
\tilde{\nu}\sim\nu
(see Lemma
4.6
) to impose the orthogonality condition localised at the scale of the stationary state

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\tilde{m}_{v}\chi_{{}_{M}}T_{0}\frac{\omega_{0}}{r}dr=0\quad\textup{with}\quad\tilde{m}_{w}(\zeta)=\tilde{m}_{v}(\zeta/\tilde{\nu}),

(49)

where
M\gg 1
is a fixed constant and the
\phi_{n,\tilde{\nu}}
’s are the eigenfunctions of the linearized operator
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}
around
Q_{\tilde{\nu}}
, defined as in (
32
) with
\nu
replaced by
\tilde{\nu}
. The orthogonality condition (
49
) allows to derive the coercivity of
\mathscr{A}_{0}
(see Lemma
B.1
). This coercivity together with the dissipation structure of the problem yield the control of
\tilde{m}_{v}
and its derivatives near the origin in the parabolic zone
|z|\lesssim 1
(or
r\ll\frac{1}{\nu}
) (see (
105
) and Lemma
4.9
), and we obtain a pointwise bound for
\tilde{m}_{v}
(see (
134
)). When using the decomposition (
48
), the linear operator
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
is changed into (see the beginning of Section
4.2
)

\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}=\frac{\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}+\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}}{2}.

and the nonlinear terms can now be estimated directly. A remarkable fact is that
\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}
adds a perturbation to
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
that avoids the resonance near the origin. As a consequence the spectral structure of
\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}
remains the same and the spectral gap still holds true, see Lemma
4.2
. We finally arrive at

\frac{d}{d\tau}\|m_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\leq-\|m_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}+C\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}},

from which (
47
) then directly follows after an integration in time.

The control of the nonradial part is greatly simplified thanks to the coercivity estimate (
36
). To measure the size of
\varepsilon^{\perp}
, we use the well-adapted norm related to (
35
),

\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{0}^{2}=\nu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{\perp}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\mathscr{M}^{z}(\varepsilon^{\perp}\sqrt{\rho_{0}})dz\sim\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\omega_{\nu}}}.

In particular, we establish the following monotonicity formula (see Lemma
4.11
)

\frac{d}{d\tau}\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|^{2}_{0}\leq-\delta^{\prime}\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|^{2}_{0}+Ce^{-2\kappa\tau}\quad\textup{for some $0<\kappa\ll 1$},

for some constant
\delta^{\prime}>0
, which gives
\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{\nu}}}\lesssim e^{-\kappa\tau}
after an integration in time.

A control on additional higher order regularity norms on the solution is also required to close the remaining nonlinear terms. We use parabolic regularity to obtain from our key decay in
L^{2}_{\omega}
decays for higher order derivatives. This is done outside the blow-up zone, where the exponentially decaying weight
\omega
cannot control the solution. In this zone however the renormalised solution is close to zero and the analysis boils down to the stability of the zero solution subject to small boundary terms. This is also done near the origin as explained previously, where the weight
\omega
does not control the solution at scale
\nu
. In this zone the renormalised solution is close to the stationary state
U_{\nu}
, and the scaling term in the dynamics is negligible. We then control the perturbation via suitable coercivity estimates, as the boundary terms coming from the parabolic zone are already controlled.

The rest of paper is organized as follows: In Section
2
, we formulate the problem and recall key properties of the linearized operator. Section
3
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.1
assuming technical details which are left to Section
4
. In Section
5
, we sketch the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

2. Linear analysis in the parabolic zone

2.1. Parabolic variables and renormalisation

Given
(\mu,x^{*})\in C^{1}([0,T),(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we introduce the parabolic variables

\displaystyle\quad z=\frac{x-x^{*}(t)}{\mu},\quad\frac{d\tau}{dt}=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}},\ \ \tau(0)=\tau_{0},

(50)

and the corresponding renormalisation

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}(t)}w(z,\tau),\quad\Phi_{u}(x,t)=\Phi_{w}(z,\tau).

The renormalisation rate is encoded by the following parameter

\beta=-\frac{\mu_{\tau}}{\mu}.

(51)

The variables (
50
) are indeed parabolic ones as we will have, once translating back to original variables, that
\beta\rightarrow\beta_{\infty}>0
and
\mu(\tau)\sim\sqrt{T-t}
from (
247
) for some blow-up time
T>0
. The problem (
7
) is transformed into the new equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}w=\nabla\cdot\big(\nabla w-w\nabla\Phi_{w}\big)-\beta\nabla\cdot(zw)+\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla w.

(52)

In the partial mass setting, and in the parabolic variables (
50
), that is, introducing

m_{w}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{B(0,\zeta)}w(z)dz,\ \ \zeta=|z|,

(53)

the corresponding equation reads as

\partial_{\tau}m_{w}=\partial_{\zeta}^{2}m_{w}-\frac{1}{\zeta}\partial_{\zeta}m_{w}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}(m_{w}^{2})}{2\zeta}-\beta\zeta\partial_{\zeta}m_{w}+N_{0}(w^{\perp}),

(54)

where for
S(0,\zeta)
the sphere at the origin with radius
\zeta
,

N_{0}(w^{\perp})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S(0,\zeta)}w^{\perp}\Big(\nabla\Phi_{w^{\perp}}-\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\Big)\cdot\vec{n}dS,

(55)

with
w^{\bot}(z)=w(z)-(2\pi\zeta)^{-1}\int_{S(0,\zeta)}wdS
the nonradial part of
w
.

2.2. Spectral analysis and coercivity for the linearised operator

Linearising equation (
54
) around the rescaled soliton
Q_{\nu}
(see (
19
)) leads to the study of the linearized operator
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
whose spectrum has been studied in details in
[
10
]
via matched asymptotic expansions. Note that in the radial setting, studying the linear operator
\mathscr{L}^{z}
is equivalent to studying
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
through the relation (
30
). In particular, if
\phi_{n,\nu}
is an eigenfunction of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
, then
\partial_{\zeta}\phi_{n,\nu}/\zeta
is a radial eigenfunction of
\mathscr{L}^{z}
. For the reader’s convenience, we recall from Proposition 1.1 of
[
10
]
the spectral properties of the operator
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
.

Proposition 2.1

(Spectral properties of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
,
[
10
]
)
.

The linear operator
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}:H^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}\to L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
is essentially self-adjoint with compact resolvant. Moreover, given any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\beta_{*}<\beta^{*}
and
0<\delta\ll 1
, then there exists
\nu^{*}>0
such that the following holds for all
0<\nu\leq\nu^{*}
and
\beta_{*}\leq\beta\leq\beta^{*}
.

(i)

(Eigenvalues)
The first
N+1
eigenvalues
\alpha_{0},...,\alpha_{N}
are given by

\alpha_{n}=2\beta\Big(1-n+\frac{1}{2\ln\nu}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\Big),\;\;\tilde{\alpha}_{n}=O\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\right).

(56)

Moreover, we have the refined estimate with
\gamma
the Euler constant:

\tilde{\alpha}_{n}=\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-n-\ln\beta}{4|\ln\nu|^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}\right)\;\textup{for}\;n=0,1.

(57)

(ii)

(Eigenfunctions)
There exist eigenfunctions
\phi_{n,\nu}
given by (
61
) with:

\langle\phi_{n,\nu},\phi_{m,\nu}\rangle^{2}_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=c_{n}\delta_{m,n},\qquad\qquad\mbox{for }n,m\leq N.

(58)

where, for some
c>0
,

c_{0}\sim\frac{|\ln\nu|}{8},\quad c_{1}\sim\frac{|\ln\nu|^{2}}{4},\quad c|\ln\nu|^{2}\leq c_{n}\leq\frac{1}{c}|\ln\nu|^{2},

We also have the pointwise estimates for
k=0,1,2
,

\displaystyle\left|D^{k}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|D^{k}\beta\partial_{\beta}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)\right|+\left|D^{k}\nu\partial_{\nu}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)\right|\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{2n-2+\delta}\big(1+\zeta^{2}\ln\big\langle\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\big\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{\{n\geq 1\}}\big)}{(\zeta+\nu)^{2+k}}.

(59)

(iii)

(Spectral gap estimate)
For any
g\in L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
belonging to the domain of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
with
\langle g,\phi_{j,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=0
for
0\leq j\leq N
, one has

\int_{0}^{\infty}g(\zeta)\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}g(\zeta)\frac{\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)}{\zeta}d\zeta\leq\alpha_{N+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{2}(\zeta)\frac{\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)}{\zeta}d\zeta.

(60)

Due to the degeneracy of the problem, one has to track precise information of the eigenfunctions, especially for the first two ones. By construction, the eigenfunctions are obtained through the following approximation

\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{n,j}\beta^{j}\nu^{2j-2}T_{j}\big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\big)+\tilde{\phi}_{n,\nu}(\zeta),

(61)

where for
\mathscr{A}_{0}
the linearised operator around the stationary state
Q
introduced in (
23
),

T_{j}=(-1)^{j}(\mathscr{A}_{0})^{-j}T_{0},\ \ T_{0}(r)=\frac{1}{8}r\partial_{r}Q(r),\ \ c_{n,j}=2^{j}\frac{n!}{(n-j)!},

(62)

and for
\psi_{0}(r)=\frac{r^{2}}{\left<r\right>^{4}}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{0}(r)=\frac{r^{4}+4r^{2}\ln r-1}{\left<r\right>^{4}}
the inverse
\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}f(r):=\frac{1}{2}\psi_{0}(r)\int_{r}^{1}\frac{\zeta^{4}+4\zeta^{2}\ln\zeta-1}{\zeta}f(\zeta)d\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\psi}_{0}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\zeta f(\zeta)d\zeta,

(63)

and
\tilde{\phi}_{n,\nu}
is a smaller order remainder described in the following.

Lemma 2.2

(Eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
,
[
10
]
)
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
2.1
, one has the following identities and upper bounds for
k=0,1,2
.

(i)
(Estimates for
\phi_{0,\nu}
)

\displaystyle|D^{k}\nu\partial_{\nu}\phi_{0,\nu}

\displaystyle|\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{2}}{(\nu+\zeta)^{2+k}},

(64)

\displaystyle|D^{k}\beta\partial_{\beta}\phi_{0,\nu}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{2}}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}},

(65)

and

|D^{k}\tilde{\phi}_{0,\nu}(\zeta)|\lesssim\frac{1}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{\zeta\lesssim\nu\}}+\frac{|\ln\zeta|}{|\ln\nu|}\mathbf{1}_{\{\zeta\gtrsim\nu\}}\right).

(66)

(ii)
(Estimates for
\phi_{1,\nu}
)
Firstly,

|D^{k}\tilde{\phi}_{1,\nu}(\zeta)|\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{4}}{|\ln\nu|(\zeta+\nu)^{k}},

(67)

and, secondly,

\nu\partial_{\nu}\phi_{1,\nu}=-2\beta(r\partial_{r}T_{1})\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\right)+R_{1},\quad\beta\partial_{\beta}\phi_{1,\nu}=\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}+R_{2},

where

|D^{k}R_{1}(\zeta)|\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{4}}{|\ln\nu|(\zeta+\nu)^{k}},

(68)

|D^{k}R_{2}(\zeta)|\lesssim\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{4}}{(\zeta+\nu)^{k}}.

(69)

(iii)
(Cancellation near the origin)
For all
0\leq n\leq N
:

|D^{k}\left(\phi_{n,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\right)|\lesssim\min\left(\nu^{2}\left\langle\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\right\rangle^{2},\frac{1}{\ln\nu}\right)\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{2n+2}}{(\zeta+\nu)^{2+k}}.

(70)

We also prove in
[
10
]
a coercivity property of the linearized operator
\mathscr{L}
acting on purely nonradial functions avoiding the direction
\nabla U
. Before stating it, note that on the one hand, in the first decomposition for
\mathscr{L}
(
21
), the operator
\mathscr{L}_{0}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
endowed with the inner product

\langle u,v\rangle_{{}_{\mathscr{M}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\mathscr{M}v\,dy,\quad\|u\|_{{}_{\mathscr{M}}}^{2}=\langle u,u\rangle_{{}_{\mathscr{M}}}.

(71)

The positivity of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{}_{\mathscr{M}}}
is subject to some suitable orthogonality conditions as showed in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3

(Coercivity of
\mathscr{M}
,
[
51
]
,
[
10
]
)
.

Let
u
be such that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|u|^{2}}{U}dy<+\infty
and
\int_{|x|=r}u=0
for almost every
r>0
. Then, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\mathscr{M}udy\geq 0,

(72)

and there exist
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},C>0
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\mathscr{M}udy\geq\delta_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{u^{2}}{U}dy-C\Big[\langle u,\Lambda U\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle u,\partial_{1}U\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle u,\partial_{2}U\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big].

(73)

If
u
additionally satisfies
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{U}dy<+\infty
, then one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla(\mathscr{M}u)|^{2}dy\geq\delta_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{U}dy-C\Big[\langle u,\partial_{1}U\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle u,\partial_{2}U\rangle_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big].

(74)

On the other hand,
\mathscr{L}
can be written the following way:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}u

\displaystyle=\mathscr{H}u-\nabla U\cdot\nabla\Phi_{u}\quad\textup{with}\quad\mathscr{H}u=\frac{1}{\omega}\nabla\cdot\Big(\omega\nabla u\Big)+2(U-b)u,

(75)

with the weight function
\omega
defined in (
26
). Not containing the nonlocal part, the operator
\mathscr{H}
is self-adjoint in the weighted
L^{2}_{\omega}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with inner product

\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}uv\omega(y)\,dy,\quad\|u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}=\langle u,u\rangle_{L^{2}_{\omega}}.

(76)

For our problem at hand, we will be able to neglect the part of the nonlocal term in
\mathscr{L}
located away from the origin. We thus introduce the following ”mixed” scalar product, with a localised Poisson field, which matches the two previous ones (
71
) and (
76
) to leading order close and away from the origin respectively:

\langle u,v\rangle_{\ast}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\sqrt{\rho}\mathscr{M}(v\sqrt{\rho})dy.

(77)

To avoid the far away contribution for the Poisson field which is not under control in
L^{2}(\omega)
, we localize the Poisson field in the linearized operator accordingly:

\tilde{\Phi}_{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}(-\Delta)^{-1}(u\sqrt{\rho}),

and consider the slightly modified operator

\tilde{\mathscr{L}}u=\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla\Phi_{U})-\nabla\cdot(U\nabla\tilde{\Phi}_{u})-b\nabla\cdot(yu).

We claim that in the non-radial sector, the localised operator
\tilde{\mathscr{L}}
is coercive for the mixed scalar product (
76
) under the natural orthogonality assumption to
\nabla U
.

Proposition 2.4

(Coercivity of
\tilde{\mathscr{L}}
,
[
10
]
)
.

There exists
\delta_{0},C>0
and
b^{*}>0
such that for all
0<b\leq b^{*}
, if
u\in\dot{H}^{1}_{\omega}
and satisfies
\int_{|y|=r}u(y)dy=0
for almost every
r\in(0,\infty)
, then we have

\langle-\tilde{\mathscr{L}}u,u\rangle_{\ast}\geq\delta_{0}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}-C\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u\partial_{i}U\sqrt{\rho}dy\right)^{2}.

(78)

Remark 2.5
.

Proposition
2.4
concerns
\tilde{\mathscr{L}}
instead of
\mathscr{L}
. However, the difference

(\mathscr{L}-\tilde{\mathscr{L}})u=\nabla U\cdot\nabla(\tilde{\Phi}_{u}-\Phi_{u}),

will be controlled by dissipative effects and the fast decay of the stationary solution
U
together with an appropriate outer norm defined in Definition
3.3
.

3. Stable blowup dynamics

3.1. Inner variables and renormalisations

The most important part of the analysis is done in parabolic variables (
50
). We will also use the inner variables

\displaystyle\quad y=\frac{z}{\nu},\quad\frac{ds}{d\tau}=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}},\ \ s(0)=s_{0},

(79)

and the corresponding renormalisation:

\displaystyle w(z,\tau)=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}(\tau)}v(y,s),\quad\Phi_{w}(z,\tau)=\Phi_{v}(y,s),

(80)

so that the problem (
52
) is transformed into the further renormalised equation

\displaystyle\partial_{s}v=\nabla\cdot\big(\nabla v-v\nabla\Phi_{v}\big)+\left(\frac{\nu_{s}}{\nu}-\nu^{2}\beta\right)\nabla\cdot(zv)+\frac{\nu x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla v.

(81)

The parameter
\nu
in (
79
) is fixed via the first orthogonality condition in (
89
). This is the orthogonal projection in
L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}
of the solution
w
onto the set of stationary states
(U_{\lambda})_{\lambda>0}
. Roughly speaking, this says that
w
is close to
U_{\nu}
in the parabolic zone. However, it will not be enough to show that in inner variables (
80
),
v
is close to
U
, with a sufficient estimate in order to control the nonlinear terms.

To cope with this issue, we use a second decomposition involving a slightly rescaled stationary state at scale
\tilde{\nu}
, fixed by another orthogonality condition (
96
). In particular, we introduce a modified parameter
\tilde{\nu}
with the associated modified inner variables for the partial mass (
53
)

m_{v}(\tilde{r},s)=m_{w}(\zeta,\tau),\quad\tilde{r}=\frac{\zeta}{\tilde{\nu}},\quad\frac{ds}{d\tau}=\frac{1}{\tilde{\nu}^{2}},

(82)

where
m_{v}
solves the new equation

\partial_{s}m_{v}=\partial_{\tilde{r}}^{2}m_{v}-\frac{1}{\tilde{r}}\partial_{\tilde{r}}m_{v}+\frac{\partial_{\tilde{r}}(m_{v}^{2})}{2\tilde{r}}+\left(\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{s}}{\tilde{\nu}}-\tilde{\nu}^{2}\beta\right)\tilde{r}\partial_{\tilde{r}}m_{v}+\tilde{N}_{0}(v^{\perp}),

(83)

where

\tilde{N}_{0}(v^{\perp})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S(0,\tilde{r})}v^{\perp}\Big(\nabla\Phi_{v^{\perp}}-\frac{x_{s}^{*}}{\mu\tilde{\nu}}\Big).\vec{n}dS.

3.2. Ansatz

3.2.1. First decomposition in parabolic variables

In parabolic variables (
50
) we take an approximate solution to (
52
) of the form

\displaystyle W=W[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](z,\tau)

\displaystyle=U_{\nu}(\zeta)+a_{1}(\tau)(\varphi_{1,\nu}-\varphi_{0,\nu})+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\varphi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)

(84)

\displaystyle\equiv U_{\nu}(\zeta)+\Psi_{1,\nu}+\Psi_{2,\nu}.

where above, we recall that the rescaled stationary state is

U_{\nu}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{\nu^{2}}U\Big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\Big)=\frac{8\nu^{2}}{(\nu^{2}+\zeta^{2})^{2}},

\nu(\tau)
,
\mathbf{a}(\tau)=\big(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\big)(\tau)
and
\beta(\tau)
are unknown functions to be determined,
(\varphi_{n,\nu})_{0\leq n\leq N}
are the radial eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{L}^{z}
defined in (
17
), and the approximate perturbation is

\Psi_{1,\nu}(\tau,\zeta)=a_{1}(\tau)(\varphi_{1,\nu}-\varphi_{0,\nu}),\quad\Psi_{2,\nu}(\tau,\zeta)=\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\varphi_{n,\nu}(\zeta).

(85)

The approximate solution to (
54
) in partial mass (
53
) corresponding to (
84
) is

m_{W}[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](\zeta,\tau)=Q_{\nu}(\zeta)+a_{1}(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu})+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)\equiv Q_{\nu}(\zeta)+P_{\nu},

(86)

where the partial mass of the stationary state
Q_{\nu}(\zeta)=m_{U_{\nu}}(\zeta)
is given by

Q_{\nu}(\zeta)=Q\Big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\Big)=\frac{4\zeta^{2}}{\nu^{2}+\zeta^{2}},

(\phi_{n,\nu})_{0\leq n\leq N}
are the renormalised eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}
defined as in (
32
), and the partial mass of the approximate perturbation is

P_{\nu}=P_{1,\nu}+P_{2,\nu},\quad P_{1,\nu}=a_{1}(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}),\quad P_{2,\nu}=\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}(\tau)\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta).

(87)

The full solutions to (
52
) and (
54
) are then decomposed as

w=W+\varepsilon,\qquad m_{w}=m_{W}+m_{\varepsilon}=Q_{\nu}+P_{1,\nu}+\bar{m}_{\varepsilon}\quad\textup{with}\quad\bar{m}_{\varepsilon}=P_{2,\nu}+m_{\varepsilon}.

(88)

Decomposing the remainder
\varepsilon
between radial part and nonradial parts (with
0
or
\perp
superscript respectively):

\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{0}+\varepsilon^{\perp},\ \ q=q^{0}+q^{\perp},

the decomposition (
88
) is ensured by the orthogonality conditions

m_{\varepsilon}\perp_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\phi_{n,\nu}\mbox{ for }0\leq n\leq N,\ \ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q^{\perp}\partial_{1}U\sqrt{\rho}dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q^{\perp}\partial_{2}U\sqrt{\rho}dy=0.

(89)

In the blow-up variables (
79
), we will use the notation
r=|y|
, and

v=V+q,\ \ V[\nu,\beta,\mathbb{a}](s,y)=U(y)+\frac{a_{1}}{\nu^{2}}\big[\varphi_{1}(r)-\varphi_{0}(r)\big]+\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{a_{n}}{\nu^{2}}\varphi_{n}(r),

(90)

where we have the relations for the remainders and eigenfunctions:

q(s,y)=\nu^{2}\varepsilon(\tau,z),\quad m_{q}(s,r)=m_{\varepsilon}(\tau,\zeta),\quad\varphi_{n}(r)=\frac{\partial_{r}\phi_{n}(r)}{r},\quad\partial_{r}\Phi_{\varphi_{n}}(r)=-\frac{\phi_{n}(r)}{r},

and:

\phi_{n}(r)=\nu^{2}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta),\ \ \varphi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)=\frac{\partial_{\zeta}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)}{\zeta},\ \ \varphi_{n}(r)=\nu^{4}\varphi_{n,\nu}(z).

3.2.2. Second decomposition in parabolic variables and inner variables

We then consider the decomposition in modified inner variables (
82
)

m_{v}(\tilde{r},s)=Q(\tilde{r})+\tilde{P}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)-\tilde{N}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)+\tilde{m}_{v}(\tilde{r},s).

(91)

Above,
\tilde{P}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)-\tilde{N}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)
is the modified approximate perturbation where

\tilde{P}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)=P_{1,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta,\tau)=a_{1}(\tau)\big(\phi_{1,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)-\phi_{0,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)\big),

(92)

with
\phi_{n,\tilde{\nu}}
being the eigenfunction, given by (
61
) with
\nu
replaced by
\tilde{\nu}
, of

\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\zeta}=\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{0}^{\zeta}-\beta\zeta\partial_{\zeta},\qquad\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{0}^{\zeta}=\partial_{\zeta}^{2}-\frac{1}{\zeta}\partial_{\zeta}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}(Q_{\tilde{\nu}}\cdot)}{\zeta},

which is the linearized operator around
Q_{\tilde{\nu}}
, and, with
\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}
given by (
63
),

\displaystyle\tilde{N}_{1}(\tilde{r},s)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial_{\tilde{r}}\tilde{P}_{1}^{2}}{2\tilde{r}}+8\beta\tilde{\nu}^{2}\tilde{\phi}_{0}\right)=\tilde{\nu}^{4}\big(\tilde{\mathscr{A}}_{0}^{\zeta}\big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\partial_{\zeta}P_{1,\tilde{\nu}}^{2}}{2\zeta}+8\beta\tilde{\phi}_{0,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)\Big):=N_{1,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta,\tau),

(93)

where
\tilde{\phi}_{0,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)=\phi_{0,\tilde{\nu}}(\zeta)-\tilde{\nu}^{-2}T_{0}(\zeta/\tilde{\nu})
satisfies the pointwise bound (
66
). The introduction of
\tilde{N}_{1}
is just a technical issue for the control of the inner norm (
125
). Roughly speaking, we want the source error term to be of size
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{4}}{|\ln\nu|}\right)
for the norm (
105
), however the error terms created by
\tilde{P}_{1}
are only of size
\mathcal{O}(\nu^{4})
on compact sets, but with strong decay at infinity. The correction
\tilde{N}_{1}
then precisely cancels these terms.

In terms of
(\zeta,\tau)
-variables, the decomposition (
91
) is equivalent to

m_{w}=Q_{\tilde{\nu}}+P_{1,\tilde{\nu}}-N_{1,\tilde{\nu}}+\tilde{m}_{w},\quad\tilde{m}_{w}(\zeta,\tau)=\tilde{m}_{v}(\tilde{r},s),

(94)

where the modified remainder is

\tilde{m}_{w}=Q_{\nu}-Q_{\tilde{\nu}}+P_{1,\nu}-P_{1,\tilde{\nu}}+N_{1,\tilde{\nu}}+\bar{m}_{\varepsilon}.

(95)

The parameter
\tilde{\nu}
is fixed by the orthogonality condition in modified inner variables:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\tilde{m}_{v}(\tilde{r})\chi_{{}_{M}}(\tilde{r})T_{0}(\tilde{r})\frac{\omega_{0}(\tilde{r})}{\tilde{r}}d\tilde{r}=0\quad\textup{for some fixed constant}\;M\gg 1,

(96)

with
\chi_{{}_{M}}
being defined as in (
15
). In particular, this orthogonality condition and the rough bound (
123
) for
m_{\varepsilon}
(see Lemma
4.6
below) ensure
\nu
and
\tilde{\nu}
are close:

\big|\nu-\tilde{\nu}\big|\lesssim\frac{\nu}{|\ln\nu|}.

3.3. The error generated by the approximate solution

We claim that
W[\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta](z,\tau)
introduced in (
84
) is a good approximate profile to (
52
) in the following sense.

Lemma 3.1

(Approximate profile)
.

Assume that
(\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta,x^{*})
are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
maps with

(\nu,\mathbf{a},\beta,x^{*}):[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\mapsto(0,\nu^{*}]\times(0,a^{*}]^{N}\times\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\beta^{*},\frac{1}{2}+\beta^{*}\Big)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

for
0<\nu^{*},a^{*},\beta^{*}\ll 1
and
1\ll\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}\leq+\infty
, with a priori bounds:

\left|\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|},\quad|a_{1}|\lesssim\nu^{2},\quad|a_{n}|\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\;\;\textup{for}\;\;n\in\{2,\cdots,N\},\quad\big|\beta_{\tau}\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{3}}.

Then the error generated by (
84
) for (
52
) is given by

\displaystyle E(z,\tau)=-\partial_{\tau}W+\nabla\cdot(\nabla W-W\nabla\Phi_{W})-\beta\nabla\cdot(zW)+\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla W\equiv\frac{\partial_{\zeta}m_{E}}{\zeta}+\frac{x^{*}_{\tau}}{\mu}\cdot\nabla W,

where

m_{E}(\zeta,\tau)=-\partial_{\tau}m_{W}-\beta\zeta\partial_{\zeta}m_{W}+\zeta\partial_{\zeta}\big(\zeta^{-1}\partial_{\zeta}m_{W}\big)+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}m_{W}^{2}}{2\zeta},

(97)

can be decomposed as

m_{E}(\zeta,\tau)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\textup{Mod}_{n}\;\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)+\tilde{m}_{E}(\zeta,\tau)+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}P_{\nu}^{2}}{2\zeta},

(98)

with

\displaystyle\textup{Mod}_{0}=\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\right)8\nu^{2}+a_{1,\tau}-a_{1}2\beta(1+\tilde{\alpha}_{0}),

(99)

\displaystyle\textup{Mod}_{n}=-\big[a_{n,\tau}-2\beta(1-n+\tilde{\alpha}_{n})a_{n}\big],

(100)

and

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{m}_{E},\phi_{0,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=-\frac{a_{1}}{8}\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}|\ln\nu|\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\right),

(101)

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{m}_{E},\phi_{1,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\frac{a_{1}}{4}|\ln\nu|\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}|\ln\nu|\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}\right),

(102)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{m}_{E}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\nu^{4}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\right),\;\langle\tilde{m}_{E},\phi_{n,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\mathcal{O}(\nu^{2})\;\;\textup{for}\;\;n\in\{2,\cdots,N\}.

(103)

Proof.

Plugging the expansion (
86
) into (
97
) yields

m_{E}(\zeta,\tau)=\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\right)\zeta\partial_{\zeta}Q_{\nu}-\partial_{\tau}P_{\nu}+\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}P_{\nu}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}P_{\nu}^{2}}{2\zeta}.

By noting that
\zeta\partial_{\zeta}Q_{\nu}\sim 8\nu^{2}\phi_{0,\nu}
and recalling from Proposition
2.1
that
\mathscr{A}^{\zeta}\phi_{n,\nu}=2\beta(1-n+\tilde{\alpha}_{n})\phi_{n,\nu}
, we write

\displaystyle m_{E}(\zeta,\tau)

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\right)8\nu^{2}+a_{1,\tau}-a_{1}2\beta(1+\tilde{\alpha}_{0})\right]\phi_{0,\nu}-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\big[a_{n,\tau}-a_{n}2\beta(1-n+\tilde{\alpha}_{n})\big]\;\phi_{n,\nu}+\tilde{m}_{E}+\frac{\partial_{\zeta}P_{\nu}^{2}}{2\zeta},

(104)

where the remainder is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{m}_{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-a_{1}\Big[\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big)+\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}\beta\partial_{\beta}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big)\Big]-\sum_{n=2}^{N}\Big[a_{n}\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\nu\partial_{\nu}\phi_{n,\nu}+\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}\beta\partial_{\beta}\phi_{n,\nu}\Big]

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}-\beta\right)\big(\zeta\partial_{\zeta}Q_{\nu}-8\nu^{2}\phi_{0,\nu}\big).

From the a priori assumptions, we remark that the leading order term in
\tilde{m}_{E}
is
a_{1}\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\big[\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big)\big]
. In particular, we have from Lemma
2.2
the identity

\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big)=-2\beta(r\partial_{r}T_{1})\big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle}{|\ln\nu|}\big)=\beta+\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle}{|\ln\nu|}\big),

from which and the asymptotic behavior
T_{1}(r)=-\frac{1}{2}\ln r+\frac{1}{4}+\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\ln r}{r^{2}}\big)
for
r\gg 1
, we compute asymptotically

\displaystyle\langle\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{0,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\beta\int_{0}^{+\infty}\phi_{0,\nu}(\zeta)\zeta^{-1}\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)d\zeta+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\frac{\beta\nu^{2}}{8}\int_{0}^{+\infty}re^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}r^{2}}{2}}dr+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|}\right)=\frac{1}{8}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|}\right),

\displaystyle\langle\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{1,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\beta\int_{0}^{+\infty}\phi_{1,\nu}(\zeta)\zeta^{-1}\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)d\zeta+\mathcal{O}(1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=-\frac{\beta^{2}\nu^{4}|\ln\nu|}{8}\int_{0}^{+\infty}r^{3}e^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}r^{2}}{2}}dr+\mathcal{O}(1)=-\frac{|\ln\nu|}{4}+\mathcal{O}(1),

and for
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\langle\nu\partial_{\nu}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{n,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\mathcal{O}(|\ln\nu|).

From Lemma
2.2
, we have the relation

\beta\partial_{\beta}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big)=2\beta T_{1}\Big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\langle\zeta\rangle^{4}),

from which and from the behavior of
T_{1}
, we compute

\displaystyle\langle\beta\partial_{\beta}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{0,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=2\beta\int_{0}^{+\infty}T_{1}\Big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\Big)\phi_{0,\nu}(\zeta)\zeta^{-1}\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)d\zeta+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}}{8}\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\ln r)\,e^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}r^{2}}{2}}rdr+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)\ =\ -\frac{|\ln\nu|}{8}+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),

\displaystyle\langle\beta\partial_{\beta}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{1,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=2\beta\int_{0}^{+\infty}T_{1}\Big(\frac{\zeta}{\nu}\Big)\phi_{1,\nu}(\zeta)\zeta^{-1}\omega_{\nu}(\zeta)d\zeta+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{\beta^{2}\nu^{4}}{8}\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\ln r)^{2}r^{2}e^{-\frac{\beta\nu^{2}r^{2}}{2}}rdr+\mathcal{O}\left(|\ln\nu|\right)\ =\ \frac{|\ln\nu|^{2}}{4}+\mathcal{O}\left(|\ln\nu|\right),

and for
n\geq 2
,

\langle\beta\partial_{\beta}\big(\phi_{1,\nu}-\phi_{0,\nu}\big),\phi_{n,\nu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\mathcal{O}(|\ln\nu|^{2}).

Using the a priori assumptions, (
70
) and (
59
), we obtain the following rough estimates:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\nu\partial_{\nu}\phi_{n,\nu}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\lesssim\sum_{n=2}^{N}\left|a_{n}\frac{\nu_{\tau}}{\nu}\right||\ln\nu|\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{n=2}^{N}a_{n}\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}\beta\partial_{\beta}\phi_{n,\nu}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\lesssim\sum_{n=2}^{N}\left|a_{n}\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\beta}\right||\ln\nu|\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{4}},

Using
\zeta\partial_{\zeta}Q_{\nu}(\zeta)=8T_{0}(r)
yields

\left\|\big(\zeta\partial_{\zeta}Q_{\nu}-8\nu^{2}\phi_{0,\nu}\big)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}=\nu^{2}\|\tilde{\phi}_{0,\nu}\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}.

The collection of the above estimates yields the estimate for
\tilde{m}_{E}
and closes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

3.4. Bootstrap regime

We describe a regime in which the solution
w
is close to the approximate solution
W
. The most important quantity is
\|m_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta})}
giving radial
L^{2}
control in the parabolic zone
\zeta\lesssim 1
, from which we are able to close the leading dynamical system driving the law of blowup solutions in Lemma
4.1
.

To close estimates at the nonlinear level, we use higher order regularity norms in the parabolic zone, that we decompose as the union of the inner zone
\zeta\leq\zeta_{*}
and of the middle range zone
\zeta_{*}\leq\zeta\leq\zeta^{*}
, where we fix two numbers

0<\zeta_{*}\ll 1\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ \zeta^{*}\gg 1.

First, we use an inner norm for the radial part in modified blow-up variables (
79
)

\|\tilde{m}_{v}\|_{\textup{in}}^{2}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big[-\tilde{\nu}^{2}\big(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\tilde{\nu}}}\tilde{m}_{v}\big)+\mathscr{A}_{0}\big(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\tilde{\nu}}}\tilde{m}_{v}\big)\Big]\mathscr{A}_{0}\big(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\tilde{\nu}}}\tilde{m}_{v}\big)\frac{\omega_{0}(\tilde{r})}{\tilde{r}}d\tilde{r}

(105)

where
\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\tilde{\nu}}}
is defined as in (
15
) and

\omega_{0}(\tilde{r})=(U(\tilde{r}))^{-1}
. Second, we use standard Sobolev norms for the full remainder
\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{2}(\zeta_{*}\lesssim\zeta\lesssim\zeta^{*})}
in the zone
\zeta_{*}\leq|z|\leq\zeta^{*}
.

The nonradial part is controlled in the parabolic zone by the norm

\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{0}^{2}=\nu^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{\perp}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\mathscr{M}^{z}\big(\varepsilon^{\perp}\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\big)dz,

\mathscr{M}^{z}
being defined by (
18
) and at a higher order regularity level in the inner zone

\|q^{\perp}\|_{\textup{in}}^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathscr{L}_{0}(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\nu}}q^{\perp})\mathscr{M}(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\nu}}q^{\perp})dy=\int U|\nabla\big(\mathscr{M}(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\nu}}q^{\perp})\big)|^{2}dy,

(106)

where
\rho
,
\mathscr{L}_{0}
and
\mathscr{M}
are defined in (
26
) and (
22
) respectively. Note the equivalence thanks to the orthogonality (
89
) and the coercivity (
73
):

\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{0}\sim\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{\nu}}}.

(107)

Finally, in the outer zone
\zeta\geq\zeta^{*}
, the full lower order perturbation is decomposed in radial and nonradial parts,

\hat{w}=w-U_{\nu}-\Psi_{1,\nu}=\Psi_{2,\nu}+\varepsilon=:\hat{w}^{0}+\hat{w}^{\perp},

(108)

and is controlled outside by an outer norm in the parabolic variables:

\|\hat{w}\|_{\textup{ex}}^{2p}=\int\left(1-\chi_{\frac{\zeta^{*}}{4}}\right)\left(||z|^{2-\frac{1}{4}}\hat{w}|+||z|^{2+\frac{3}{4}}\nabla\hat{w}|\right)^{2p}\frac{dz}{|z|^{2}}\quad\textup{for some}\;\;p\gg 1.

Definition 3.2

(Bootstrap Initiation)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\kappa>0
,
\tau_{0}\gg 1
,
\mu_{0}=e^{-\tau_{0}/2}
. We say that
w_{0}
satisfies the initial bootstrap conditions if there exists
x^{*}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\beta_{0}>0
and
\nu_{0}>0
such that the following holds true. In the variables (
50
) one has the decomposition (
88
) with the orthogonality conditions (
89
) and the following estimates:

(i)

(Compatibility condition for the initial renormalisation rate
\beta_{0}
)

\frac{a_{1}}{4\nu^{2}}=-1+\frac{1}{2\ln\nu_{0}}+\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-1-\ln\beta_{0}}{4|\ln\nu_{0}|^{2}}.

(ii)

(Initial modulation parameters)
For
\bar{c}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta_{0}}}e^{-\frac{2+\gamma}{2}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{0}e^{-\sqrt{\beta_{0}\tau_{0}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{|\ln\nu_{0}|}\right)\leq\nu_{0}\leq\bar{c}_{0}e^{-\sqrt{\beta_{0}\tau_{0}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{|\ln\nu_{0}|}\right),

(109)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{|\ln\nu_{0}|}<\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{|\ln\nu_{0}|},\quad|a_{n}|<\frac{\nu^{2}_{0}}{|\ln\nu_{0}|^{2}}\ \mbox{ for }2\leq n\leq N.

(110)

(iii)

(Initial remainder)

\displaystyle\nu_{0}^{2}\|m_{q}(s_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega}{r}}}=\|m_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}<\frac{\nu^{2}_{0}}{|\ln\nu_{0}|},

(111)

\displaystyle\|m_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{2}(\frac{\zeta_{*}}{4}\leq\zeta\leq 4\zeta^{*})}<\frac{\nu_{0}^{2}}{|\ln\nu_{0}|},

(112)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{m}_{v}\|_{\textup{in}}<\frac{\nu_{0}^{2}}{|\ln\nu_{0}|},

(113)

and

\displaystyle\|\hat{w}^{0}\|_{\textup{ex}}<\frac{\nu_{0}^{2}}{|\ln\nu_{0}|},

(115)

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{0}+\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{H^{2}(\frac{\zeta_{*}}{4}\leq|z|\leq 4\zeta^{*})}<e^{-\kappa\tau_{0}},

(116)

\displaystyle\|q^{\perp}\|_{\textup{in}}<\frac{e^{-\kappa\tau_{0}}}{\nu_{0}},

(117)

\displaystyle\|\hat{w}^{\perp}\|_{\textup{ex}}<e^{-\kappa\tau_{0}}.

(118)

Our goal is to prove that solutions satisfying the initial bootstrap conditions defined by the previous definition will stay close to the approximate solution forward in time, in the following sense.

Definition 3.3

(Bootstrap)
.

Let
\kappa>0
,
\tau_{0}\gg 1
, and
K^{\prime\prime}\gg K^{\prime}\gg K\gg 1
. A solution
w
to (
1
) is said to be trapped on
[\tau_{0},\tau^{*}]
if it satisfies the initial bootstrap conditions in the sense of Definition
3.2
at time
\tau_{0}
and the following conditions on
(\tau_{0},\tau^{*}]
. There exists
\mu\in C^{1}([0,t^{*}],(0,\infty))
such that the solution can be decomposed according to (
88
), (
89
) on
[\tau_{0},\tau^{*}]
with:

(i)

(Compatibility condition for the renormalisation rate
\beta
)

\frac{a_{1}}{4\nu^{2}}=-1+\frac{1}{2\ln\nu}+\frac{\ln 2-\gamma-1-\ln\beta}{4|\ln\nu|^{2}}.

(119)

(ii)

(Modulation parameters)
For
\bar{c}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}e^{-\frac{2+\gamma}{2}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{c}e^{-\sqrt{\beta_{0}\tau+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\beta}}\left(1-\frac{K^{\prime}\ln|\ln\nu|}{|\ln\nu|}\right)\leq\nu\leq\bar{c}e^{-\sqrt{\beta_{0}\tau+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\beta}}\left(1+\frac{K^{\prime}\ln|\ln\nu|}{|\ln\nu|}\right),

(120)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-\frac{K^{\prime}}{|\ln\nu|}<\beta<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{K^{\prime}}{|\ln\nu|},

(121)

\displaystyle|a_{n}|<\frac{K\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\ \mbox{ for }2\leq n\leq N.

(122)

(iii)

(Remainder)

\displaystyle\nu^{2}\|m_{q}(s)\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega}{r}}}=\|m_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{\frac{\omega_{\nu}}{\zeta}}}<\frac{K\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|},

(123)

\displaystyle\|m_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\|_{H^{2}(\zeta_{*}\leq\zeta\leq\zeta^{*})}<\frac{K^{\prime}\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|},

(124)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{m}_{v}\|_{\textup{in}}<\frac{K^{\prime\prime}\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|},

(125)

\displaystyle\|\hat{w}^{0}\|_{\textup{ex}}<K^{\prime\prime}\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|},

(126)

and

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{0}<Ke^{-\kappa\tau},

(127)

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon^{\perp}\|_{H^{2}(\zeta_{*}\leq|z|\leq\zeta^{*})}<K^{\prime}e^{-\kappa\tau},

(128)

\displaystyle\|q^{\perp}\|_{\textup{in}}<K^{\prime\prime}\frac{e^{-\kappa\tau}}{\nu},

(129)

\displaystyle\|\hat{w}^{\perp}\|_{\textup{ex}}<K^{\prime\prime}e^{-\kappa\tau}.

(130)

The initial bootstrap conditions define an open set in which trajectories are trapped, in the sense of the following Proposition.

Proposition 3.4

(Exitence of a solution trapped in the bootstrap regime)
.

There exists a choice of the constants
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\kappa\ll 1
,
K\gg 1
,
K^{\prime}\gg 1
,
K^{\prime\prime}\gg 1
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
such that any solution which is initially in the bootstrap in the sense of Definition
3.2
will be trapped on
[\tau_{0},\infty)
in the sense of Definition
3.3
.

Proof.

This is the heart of the present paper. The next Lemmas and Propositions prepare for its proof finally done in Subsection
4.7
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The constants are determined in the following order. First,
N
is chosen large enough and
\kappa
small enough so that constants in time derivatives of Lyapunov functionals have correct signs. Then
K
is chosen first, followed by
K^{\prime}
depending on
K
, and
K^{\prime\prime}
depending on
K,K^{\prime}
. Finally,
\tau_{0}
is chosen last, so that this parameter is always adjusted throughout the proof to obtain various smallness.

3.5. Properties of the bootstrap regime

The following lemma gives some properties of a solution trapped in the bootstrap regime.

Lemma 3.6

(A priori control in the bootstrap)
.

Let
w
be a solution in the bootstrap regime in the sense of Definition
3.3
. Then for
\tau_{0}
large enough depending on
\kappa,K,K^{\prime},K^{\prime\prime}
the following estimates hold on
[\tau_{0},\tau^{*}]
:

•

(Estimate on
\nu
)

\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{2}\leq|\ln\nu|\leq\sqrt{\tau}.

(131)

•

(Estimate on the approximate perturbation) For
C
independent on the bootstrap constants
\kappa,K,K^{\prime},K^{\prime\prime}
for
k=0,1,2
:

\displaystyle|D^{k}P_{1,\nu}(\zeta)|\lesssim\nu^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\ln\langle r\rangle\rangle}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}},

(132)

\displaystyle\quad|D^{k}P_{2,\nu}(\zeta)|\lesssim\frac{1}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{\langle\zeta\rangle^{C(N)}\langle\ln\langle r\rangle\rangle}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}}.

(133)

•

(Refined pointwise estimate in the parabolic zone) For
\zeta\leq\zeta^{*}/2
:

\big|\tilde{m}_{w}(\zeta)\big|+\big|\zeta\partial_{\zeta}\tilde{m}_{w}(\zeta)\big|\lesssim\frac{\zeta^{2}}{(\zeta+\nu)^{2}}\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}\sqrt{\langle\ln\langle r\rangle\rangle}.

(134)

•

(Pointwise estimates in the outer zone) For
\zeta=|z|\geq\zeta^{*}/2
, for the full perturbation:

\big|\Psi_{\nu}+\varepsilon^{0}\big|\lesssim\nu^{2}|\ln\nu||z|^{-2+\frac{1}{4}},\quad\big|P_{\nu}+m_{\varepsilon}\big|+\big|\zeta\partial_{\zeta}(P_{\nu}+m_{\varepsilon})\big|\lesssim|\ln\nu|\nu^{2}\zeta^{\frac{1}{4}},

(135)

and for the higher order part of the perturbation:

\big|\Psi_{2,\nu}+\varepsilon^{0}\big|\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}|z|^{-2+\frac{1}{4}},\;\big|P_{2,\nu}+m_{\varepsilon}\big|+\big|\zeta\partial_{\zeta}(P_{2,\nu}+m_{\varepsilon})\big|\lesssim\frac{\nu^{2}}{|\ln\nu|}\zeta^{\frac{1}{4}},

(136)

and for the nonradial part:

|\varepsilon^{\perp}(z)|\lesssim e^{-\kappa\tau}(1+|z|)^{-2+\frac{1}{4}}.

(137)

•

(Pointwise estimate on the nonradial Poisson field) For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

|\Phi_{\varepsilon^{\perp}}(z)|+(1+|z|)|\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon^{\perp}}(z)|\lesssim(1+|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{e^{-\kappa\tau}}{\nu^{3}}.

(138)

Proof.

Note that (
131
) is a direct consequence of (
120
).

Step 1

The approximate perturbation
. From (
87
), the pointwise estimate (
70
) and the bootstrap bounds (
119
), (
120
) and (
122
), there holds for a constant
C
depending on
N
only,

\displaystyle\left|D^{k}P_{1,\nu}(\zeta)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|a_{1}||D^{k}(\phi_{1,\nu}(\zeta)-\phi_{0,\nu}(\zeta))\leq C\nu^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\frac{1+\ln\langle\frac{|z|}{\nu}\rangle}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}},

\displaystyle\left|D^{k}P_{2,\nu}(\zeta)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=2}^{N}|a_{n}|\big|D^{k}\phi_{n,\nu}(\zeta)\big|\leq\frac{C}{|\ln\nu|^{2}}\left(\frac{\zeta}{\nu+\zeta}\right)^{2-k\wedge 2}\langle\zeta\rangle^{C}\frac{1+\ln\langle\frac{|z|}{\nu}\rangle}{(\nu+\zeta)^{k}},

which are the pointwise estimates (
132
) and (
133
).

Step 2

Pointwise estimates for
\tilde{m}_{w}
. Let
f(r)=\mathscr{A}_{0}\tilde{m}_{v}
and
\tilde{m}_{w}=\mathscr{A}_{0}^{-1}f
be defined by the formula (
63
). Consider the zone
r\leq\zeta^{*}/2\nu
. Note that one has

f(r)=\mathscr{A}_{0}(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\nu}}\tilde{m}_{v})\quad\mbox{ for }\;\;r\leq\frac{2\zeta_{*}}{\nu},

and for
2\zeta_{*}\leq r\nu\leq\zeta^{*}
,

|f(r)|=|\mathscr{A}_{0}\tilde{m}_{v}(r)|\lesssim|\partial_{rr}\tilde{m}_{v}|+\nu|\partial_{r}\tilde{m}_{v}|+\nu^{2}|\tilde{m}_{v}|=\nu^{2}(|\partial_{\zeta\zeta}\tilde{m}_{w}|+|\partial_{\zeta}\tilde{m}_{w}|+|\tilde{m}_{w}|).

Hence, from the bootstrap bounds (
124
), (
125
) and the relation
\tilde{m}_{w}=Q_{\nu}-Q_{\tilde{\nu}}+P_{1,\tilde{\nu}}-P_{1,\nu}+P_{2,\nu}+m_{\varepsilon}
, we estimate

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{0}}(r\leq\frac{\zeta^{*}}{2\nu})}\lesssim\|\mathscr{A}_{0}(\chi_{\frac{\zeta_{*}}{\nu}}\tilde{m}_{v})\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{0}}}+\nu^{2}\||\partial_{\zeta\zeta}\tilde{m}_{w}|+|\partial_{\zeta}\tilde{m}_{w}|+|\tilde{m}_{w}|\|_{L^{2}_{\omega_{0}}(\frac{2\zeta_{*}}{\nu}\leq r\leq\frac{\zeta^{*}}{2\nu})}
